Yeah… yes. It’s not just one thing. My mind kind of jumps tracks all day.

Like I’ll start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again—well, not hired, I mean, whether I’ve somehow made myself unemployable if something goes wrong at school—and then it turns into, “Have I ruined my reputation with the parents?” And that reminds me of this one dad who barely said hello at pickup last week, which probably meant nothing, but I kept replaying it.

Then I’m thinking about retirement, whether I’ve let that slip away somehow, and then suddenly I’m back to something tiny from story time, like if I used the wrong tone or missed a kid’s reaction, and my brain says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where it goes. More days than not, definitely.

circling from whether I'll ever get hired again to whether I've ruined my reputation with parents, whether retirement is slipping away, and even whether a small mistake in a story-time activity means People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve had that, no. Not like watching myself from outside my body.

I mean, sometimes I get so caught up in what I’m doing that I’ll sort of run on autopilot for a minute—like tying a kid’s shoe while also trying to remember who still needs sunscreen, or where I put the attendance sheet, and then somehow I’m across the room with the glue sticks in my hand. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re describing—like looking through glass or fog—but I can’t really remember that happening to me.

Sometimes in the classroom, if the fluorescent lights are buzzing and the kids are all talking at once, I can feel sort of… worn thin, I guess. Like I’m focusing on one thing and missing three others, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I forgot where I put the markers—actually, last week I put them in the art cabinet and then spent ten minutes looking in the supply bin, which is ridiculous.

But that’s not the world seeming not real. It’s just me being scattered or overstimulated. So, no, not distant or unreal like that.

It’s more like I’m distracted or overloaded, not outside myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was somehow distant or not quite real, even for just a few seconds—like you were looking at everything through a fog or a glass window?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re describing—like looking through glass or fog—but I can’t really remember that happening to me.\n\nSometimes in the classroom, if the fluorescent lights are buzzing and the kids are all talking at once, I can feel sort of… worn thin, I guess. Like I’m focusing on one thing and missing three others, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I forgot where I put the markers—actually, last week I put them in the art cabinet and then spent ten minutes looking in the supply bin, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut that’s not the world seeming not real. It’s just me being scattered or overstimulated. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah—well, I don’t know if this counts exactly, but the big thing has been my shoulders and neck. They just stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends.

And it’s funny, because actual observations are usually fine—I mean, stressful, but fine—and I’ve been teaching long enough that you’d think I wouldn’t react like that. But even on ordinary days, I catch myself with my shoulders up around my ears. By the time I get home, my jaw aches too, and sometimes it’s bad enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar, which is one of the few things that usually helps me settle down.

So yes, that tension is honestly part of what finally pushed me to come in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… besides the shoulders and neck, I don’t think there’s anything else like that that’s really persisted.

I mean, I’ve had the usual things people get, I guess, where you wonder if something is wrong and then it turns out to be nothing, but nothing where I’ve gone through a bunch of tests and they still couldn’t explain it. The tension is the thing that sticks around.

Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I just know once you start paying attention to your body, everything can feel like it might mean something—especially when you’re around little kids all day and someone is always announcing they have a stomachache five minutes after snack. But for me, no, not really anything else that’s ongoing besides that tightness.

I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in that way.

I’m sorry, I know you’re asking it a bit specifically, and I keep sort of circling the same answer.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m tired or distracted and the room feels… not unreal, but just too much, if that makes sense. Like at school, sometimes the noise bounces around in this awful way, and I’ll be thinking, “Did I put the scissors away? Did someone’s grandmother need that form?” But I still know where I am and everything feels real. It’s more like I’m frazzled than like the world has gone strange. So… no, I don’t think I’ve felt that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a moment where you felt disconnected from your body, like you were watching yourself from outside, or felt like your surroundings were distorted or dreamlike, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. Not like watching myself from outside my body.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get so caught up in what I’m doing that I’ll sort of run on autopilot for a minute—like tying a kid’s shoe while also trying to remember who still needs sunscreen, or where I put the attendance sheet, and then somehow I’m across the room with the glue sticks in my hand. But that’s just… being busy, I think. That happens in a preschool room all the time, honestly, because there are twelve things happening at once and someone is usually crying about a mitten.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say my surroundings get dreamlike or distorted. I’m there. I know I’m there. It’s more like I’m distracted or overloaded, not outside myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was somehow distant or not quite real, even for just a few seconds—like you were looking at everything through a fog or a glass window?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re describing—like looking through glass or fog—but I can’t really remember that happening to me.\n\nSometimes in the classroom, if the fluorescent lights are buzzing and the kids are all talking at once, I can feel sort of… worn thin, I guess. Like I’m focusing on one thing and missing three others, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I forgot where I put the markers—actually, last week I put them in the art cabinet and then spent ten minutes looking in the supply bin, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut that’s not the world seeming not real. The tension is the thing that sticks around.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-explaining. It’s more frustrating than frightening, if that makes sense. Like my body won’t stop bracing, but I’m not usually thinking, “Oh no, this must be some terrible illness.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so… not like that.

Or at school, when the kids are all talking at once and I’m tying a shoe and someone needs help with a zipper, it can feel a little like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nBut outside my body, like watching myself from the ceiling or the room looking unreal… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not that I’d recognize as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden moments where your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're afraid something terrible is going to happen, even when there's no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that sudden, out-of-nowhere way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, there are plenty of times I get worked up and my mind goes straight to, “Oh no, what if this turns into a disaster,” but it usually feels tied to something—like a parent email, or something at school, or some problem I’ve been chewing on. Not like I’m just sitting there fine and then suddenly my heart is racing and I can’t breathe for no reason.\n\nAt least… I don’t think I’ve had that. I might be missing what you mean, but I wouldn’t describe it as these sudden attacks, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you seemed unreal, or like things weren’t quite real, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you asked something similar, and I keep trying to think of an example, but I don’t think I’ve had that “nothing is real” feeling.\n\nSometimes a classroom can feel chaotic in a way where I’m not fully taking in everything—like there’s glitter on the floor and three kids asking me things at once, and I’m thinking about whether I remembered to send the lunch note home—but that’s more being distracted or overloaded, I think. Not like the world looks fake or unreal. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you seemed unreal, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m still not coming up with that, no. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking it a bit specifically, and I keep sort of circling the same answer.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m tired or distracted and the room feels… not unreal, but just too much, if that makes sense. Like at school, sometimes the noise bounces around in this awful way, and I’ll be thinking, “Did I put the scissors away? Did someone’s grandmother need that form?” But I still know where I am and everything feels real. It’s more like I’m frazzled than like the world has gone strange. So… no, I don’t think I’ve felt that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a moment where you felt disconnected from your body, like you were watching yourself from outside, or felt like your surroundings were distorted or dreamlike, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. Not like watching myself from outside my body.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get so caught up in what I’m doing that I’ll sort of run on autopilot for a minute—like tying a kid’s shoe while also trying to remember who still needs sunscreen, or where I put the attendance sheet, and then somehow I’m across the room with the glue sticks in my hand. But that’s just… being busy, I think. That happens in a preschool room all the time, honestly, because there are twelve things happening at once and someone is usually crying about a mitten.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say my surroundings get dreamlike or distorted. I’m there. I know I’m there. It’s more like I’m distracted or overloaded, not outside myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was somehow distant or not quite real, even for just a few seconds—like you were looking at everything through a fog or a glass window?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re describing—like looking through glass or fog—but I can’t really remember that happening to me.\n\nSometimes in the classroom, if the fluorescent lights are buzzing and the kids are all talking at once, I can feel sort of… worn thin, I guess. Like I’m focusing on one thing and missing three others, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I forgot where I put the markers—actually, last week I put them in the art cabinet and then spent ten minutes looking in the supply bin, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut that’s not the world seeming not real. It’s just me being scattered or overstimulated. So, no, not distant or unreal like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other physical symptoms that persist, even after medical evaluations or tests have shown no clear medical cause for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah—well, I don’t know if this counts exactly, but the big thing has been my shoulders and neck. They just stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends.\n\nAnd it’s funny, because actual observations are usually fine—I mean, stressful, but fine—and I’ve been teaching long enough that you’d think I wouldn’t react like that. But even on ordinary days, I catch myself with my shoulders up around my ears. By the time I get home, my jaw aches too, and sometimes it’s bad enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar, which is one of the few things that usually helps me settle down.\n\nSo yes, that tension is honestly part of what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other physical symptoms, besides the tightness in your shoulders and neck, that have persisted even after seeing a doctor and having tests done that didn’t find a clear medical explanation?\n\nPatient: Um… besides the shoulders and neck, I don’t think there’s anything else like that that’s really persisted.\n\nI mean, I’ve had the usual things people get, I guess, where you wonder if something is wrong and then it turns out to be nothing, but nothing where I’ve gone through a bunch of tests and they still couldn’t explain it. The tension is the thing that sticks around.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I just know once you start paying attention to your body, everything can feel like it might mean something—especially when you’re around little kids all day and someone is always announcing they have a stomachache five minutes after snack. But for me, no, not really anything else that’s ongoing besides that tightness.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a lot of anxiety or concern about your physical symptoms, like worrying they might mean something more serious even when doctors haven’t found a clear explanation for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in that way.\n\nI mean, I can get fixated on it for a minute, like, “Why am I still so tense?” and then I’ll start thinking about whether I’m doing something wrong—sitting wrong, sleeping wrong, carrying too many bins at school, that kind of thing. I actually bought one of those heat wraps after seeing another teacher use one during nap time, which is probably not the point of your question.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been scared it’s something serious after a doctor said they weren’t finding anything. It’s more frustrating than frightening, if that makes sense. Like my body won’t stop bracing, but I’m not usually thinking, “Oh no, this must be some terrible illness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling especially anxious or worried specifically about your physical symptoms, like your tightness, even when doctors haven’t found a serious cause for them?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least I don’t think that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI get annoyed by it, and sometimes I sort of chase it around in my head—like, “Am I sitting funny? Did I sleep wrong? Is this from bending over tiny tables all day?” You know, those preschool chairs are basically designed to ruin an adult spine, not that that’s a medical theory or anything.\n\nBut I’m not usually worrying that the tightness means something serious. It’s more like… why can’t I just relax my body for five minutes? That’s the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that your physical symptoms, like the tightness in your shoulders and neck, might mean you have a serious medical condition that hasn’t been diagnosed yet, even after doctors have told you they can’t find anything serious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that.\n\nI mean, I can spiral a little in a general way, because once you notice your shoulders are up around your ears all day, you start thinking, “Okay, what am I doing to myself?” But I’m not usually sitting there thinking there’s some hidden serious diagnosis that everyone missed.\n\nIt’s more… I wish I could unclench. Like even when I’m trying to relax, my body doesn’t seem to get the message. And then of course I’ll get distracted wondering if I need a better pillow or if those tiny classroom chairs should come with a warning label for adults. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m worried it’s an undiagnosed serious condition.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a lot of anxiety or concern about your physical symptoms, like worrying they might mean something more serious even when doctors haven’t found a clear explanation for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in that way.\n\nI mean, I can get fixated on it for a minute, like, “Why am I still so tense?” and then I’ll start thinking about whether I’m doing something wrong—sitting wrong, sleeping wrong, carrying too many bins at school, that kind of thing. I actually bought one of those heat wraps after seeing another teacher use one during nap time, which is probably not the point of your question.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been scared it’s something serious after a doctor said they weren’t finding anything. It’s more frustrating than frightening, if that makes sense. Like my body won’t stop bracing, but I’m not usually thinking, “Oh no, this must be some terrible illness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling especially anxious or worried specifically about your physical symptoms, like your tightness, even when doctors haven’t found a serious cause for them?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least I don’t think that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI get annoyed by it, and sometimes I sort of chase it around in my head—like, “Am I sitting funny? Did I sleep wrong? Is this from bending over tiny tables all day?” You know, those preschool chairs are basically designed to ruin an adult spine, not that that’s a medical theory or anything.\n\nBut I’m not usually worrying that the tightness means something serious. It’s more like… why can’t I just relax my body for five minutes? That’s the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that your physical symptoms, like the tightness in your shoulders and neck, might mean you have a serious medical condition that hasn’t been diagnosed yet, even after doctors have told you they can’t find anything serious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that.\n\nI mean, I can spiral a little in a general way, because once you notice your shoulders are up around your ears all day, you start thinking, “Okay, what am I doing to myself?” But I’m not usually sitting there thinking there’s some hidden serious diagnosis that everyone missed.\n\nIt’s more… I wish I could unclench. Like even when I’m trying to relax, my body doesn’t seem to get the message. And then of course I’ll get distracted wondering if I need a better pillow or if those tiny classroom chairs should come with a warning label for adults. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m worried it’s an undiagnosed serious condition.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about your physical symptoms, like how serious they might be or what they could mean, even when doctors have said there’s no major issue?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I spend a lot of time on what it “means,” exactly. I notice it, sure, and then sometimes I’ll get sidetracked trying to figure out if it’s from how I’m sitting or how I’m moving around at work.

Like, I’ll be on the floor with the kids doing blocks, and then later I’m thinking, “Well, no wonder my body’s mad at me,” because those little chairs—honestly, they should issue adults knee pads or something. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

But no, I’m not sitting there for hours thinking it might be serious or anything like that. It’s more like it pops into my head, I get irritated by it, and then I move on to whatever else is happening.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what finally got me to make the appointment is this constant tightness. My shoulders and neck are just clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nI teach preschool, so there’s always noise and movement and someone needing something, and—sorry, I’m getting off track—but by the time I get home, my jaw aches so badly that I can barely enjoy playing guitar, which is usually the thing that helps me unwind. It’s just… I feel like my body won’t let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a number of different things—maybe work, relationships, or other responsibilities—more days than not, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just one thing. My mind kind of jumps tracks all day.\n\nLike I’ll start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again—well, not hired, I mean, whether I’ve somehow made myself unemployable if something goes wrong at school—and then it turns into, “Have I ruined my reputation with the parents?” And that reminds me of this one dad who barely said hello at pickup last week, which probably meant nothing, but I kept replaying it.\n\nThen I’m thinking about retirement, whether I’ve let that slip away somehow, and then suddenly I’m back to something tiny from story time, like if I used the wrong tone or missed a kid’s reaction, and my brain says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where it goes. More days than not, definitely.\n\nDoctor: How much trouble do you have controlling these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Honestly, a lot. I’ll try to rein it in—like, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, make a list, just do the next practical thing,” or I’ll pray for a minute, or go out and weed the garden because there’s always something out there, especially with the crabgrass by the fence—\n\nBut even then, it doesn’t really stop. One worry sort of hands the baton to the next one, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon in my head. It’s like I’m trying to steer it, but it keeps taking exits I didn’t mean to take.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical symptoms—like stomach issues, dizziness, or shortness of breath—that come up along with the tension or worry, even if they don’t last long?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice the tightness more than anything, because it’s just… loud, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I wonder if other things are happening and I’m just not paying attention because in a preschool room you kind of tune out your own body—like someone’s spilled paint, someone’s crying about a block tower, and you just keep moving. But stomach stuff or dizziness or breathing… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or like you're observing yourself from outside your body, or felt like the world around you seems unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I’m sort of on autopilot, like driving home and then realizing I don’t remember the last few turns, but I think everybody does that, right? Or at school, when the kids are all talking at once and I’m tying a shoe and someone needs help with a zipper, it can feel a little like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nBut outside my body, like watching myself from the ceiling or the room looking unreal… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not that I’d recognize as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden moments where your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're afraid something terrible is going to happen, even when there's no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that sudden, out-of-nowhere way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, there are plenty of times I get worked up and my mind goes straight to, “Oh no, what if this turns into a disaster,” but it usually feels tied to something—like a parent email, or something at school, or some problem I’ve been chewing on. Not like I’m just sitting there fine and then suddenly my heart is racing and I can’t breathe for no reason.\n\nAt least… I don’t think I’ve had that. I might be missing what you mean, but I wouldn’t describe it as these sudden attacks, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you seemed unreal, or like things weren’t quite real, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you asked something similar, and I keep trying to think of an example, but I don’t think I’ve had that “nothing is real” feeling.\n\nSometimes a classroom can feel chaotic in a way where I’m not fully taking in everything—like there’s glitter on the floor and three kids asking me things at once, and I’m thinking about whether I remembered to send the lunch note home—but that’s more being distracted or overloaded, I think. Not like the world looks fake or unreal. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you seemed unreal, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m still not coming up with that, no. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking it a bit specifically, and I keep sort of circling the same answer.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m tired or distracted and the room feels… not unreal, but just too much, if that makes sense. Like at school, sometimes the noise bounces around in this awful way, and I’ll be thinking, “Did I put the scissors away? Did someone’s grandmother need that form?” But I still know where I am and everything feels real. It’s more like I’m frazzled than like the world has gone strange. So… no, I don’t think I’ve felt that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a moment where you felt disconnected from your body, like you were watching yourself from outside, or felt like your surroundings were distorted or dreamlike, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. Not like watching myself from outside my body.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get so caught up in what I’m doing that I’ll sort of run on autopilot for a minute—like tying a kid’s shoe while also trying to remember who still needs sunscreen, or where I put the attendance sheet, and then somehow I’m across the room with the glue sticks in my hand. But that’s just… being busy, I think. That happens in a preschool room all the time, honestly, because there are twelve things happening at once and someone is usually crying about a mitten.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say my surroundings get dreamlike or distorted. I’m there. I know I’m there. It’s more like I’m distracted or overloaded, not outside myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was somehow distant or not quite real, even for just a few seconds—like you were looking at everything through a fog or a glass window?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re describing—like looking through glass or fog—but I can’t really remember that happening to me.\n\nSometimes in the classroom, if the fluorescent lights are buzzing and the kids are all talking at once, I can feel sort of… worn thin, I guess. Like I’m focusing on one thing and missing three others, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I forgot where I put the markers—actually, last week I put them in the art cabinet and then spent ten minutes looking in the supply bin, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut that’s not the world seeming not real. It’s just me being scattered or overstimulated. So, no, not distant or unreal like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other physical symptoms that persist, even after medical evaluations or tests have shown no clear medical cause for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah—well, I don’t know if this counts exactly, but the big thing has been my shoulders and neck. They just stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends.\n\nAnd it’s funny, because actual observations are usually fine—I mean, stressful, but fine—and I’ve been teaching long enough that you’d think I wouldn’t react like that. The tension is the thing that sticks around.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I’m not sure I’d call it fear, exactly. I know you asked that already, and I’m trying to answer it the right way.

Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what finally got me to make the appointment is this constant tightness. My shoulders and neck are just clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nI teach preschool, so there’s always noise and movement and someone needing something, and—sorry, I’m getting off track—but by the time I get home, my jaw aches so badly that I can barely enjoy playing guitar, which is usually the thing that helps me unwind. It’s just… I feel like my body won’t let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a number of different things—maybe work, relationships, or other responsibilities—more days than not, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just one thing. My mind kind of jumps tracks all day.\n\nLike I’ll start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again—well, not hired, I mean, whether I’ve somehow made myself unemployable if something goes wrong at school—and then it turns into, “Have I ruined my reputation with the parents?” And that reminds me of this one dad who barely said hello at pickup last week, which probably meant nothing, but I kept replaying it.\n\nThen I’m thinking about retirement, whether I’ve let that slip away somehow, and then suddenly I’m back to something tiny from story time, like if I used the wrong tone or missed a kid’s reaction, and my brain says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where it goes. More days than not, definitely.\n\nDoctor: How much trouble do you have controlling these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Honestly, a lot. I’ll try to rein it in—like, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, make a list, just do the next practical thing,” or I’ll pray for a minute, or go out and weed the garden because there’s always something out there, especially with the crabgrass by the fence—\n\nBut even then, it doesn’t really stop. One worry sort of hands the baton to the next one, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon in my head. It’s like I’m trying to steer it, but it keeps taking exits I didn’t mean to take.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical symptoms—like stomach issues, dizziness, or shortness of breath—that come up along with the tension or worry, even if they don’t last long?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice the tightness more than anything, because it’s just… loud, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I wonder if other things are happening and I’m just not paying attention because in a preschool room you kind of tune out your own body—like someone’s spilled paint, someone’s crying about a block tower, and you just keep moving. But stomach stuff or dizziness or breathing… I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or like you're observing yourself from outside your body, or felt like the world around you seems unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I’m sort of on autopilot, like driving home and then realizing I don’t remember the last few turns, but I think everybody does that, right? Or at school, when the kids are all talking at once and I’m tying a shoe and someone needs help with a zipper, it can feel a little like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nBut outside my body, like watching myself from the ceiling or the room looking unreal… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not that I’d recognize as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden moments where your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're afraid something terrible is going to happen, even when there's no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that sudden, out-of-nowhere way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, there are plenty of times I get worked up and my mind goes straight to, “Oh no, what if this turns into a disaster,” but it usually feels tied to something—like a parent email, or something at school, or some problem I’ve been chewing on. Not like I’m just sitting there fine and then suddenly my heart is racing and I can’t breathe for no reason.\n\nAt least… I don’t think I’ve had that. I might be missing what you mean, but I wouldn’t describe it as these sudden attacks, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you seemed unreal, or like things weren’t quite real, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you asked something similar, and I keep trying to think of an example, but I don’t think I’ve had that “nothing is real” feeling.\n\nSometimes a classroom can feel chaotic in a way where I’m not fully taking in everything—like there’s glitter on the floor and three kids asking me things at once, and I’m thinking about whether I remembered to send the lunch note home—but that’s more being distracted or overloaded, I think. Not like the world looks fake or unreal. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you seemed unreal, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m still not coming up with that, no. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking it a bit specifically, and I keep sort of circling the same answer.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m tired or distracted and the room feels… not unreal, but just too much, if that makes sense. Like at school, sometimes the noise bounces around in this awful way, and I’ll be thinking, “Did I put the scissors away? Did someone’s grandmother need that form?” But I still know where I am and everything feels real. It’s more like I’m frazzled than like the world has gone strange. So… no, I don’t think I’ve felt that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a moment where you felt disconnected from your body, like you were watching yourself from outside, or felt like your surroundings were distorted or dreamlike, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. Not like watching myself from outside my body.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get so caught up in what I’m doing that I’ll sort of run on autopilot for a minute—like tying a kid’s shoe while also trying to remember who still needs sunscreen, or where I put the attendance sheet, and then somehow I’m across the room with the glue sticks in my hand. But that’s just… being busy, I think. That happens in a preschool room all the time, honestly, because there are twelve things happening at once and someone is usually crying about a mitten.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say my surroundings get dreamlike or distorted. I’m there. I know I’m there. It’s more like I’m distracted or overloaded, not outside myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was somehow distant or not quite real, even for just a few seconds—like you were looking at everything through a fog or a glass window?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re describing—like looking through glass or fog—but I can’t really remember that happening to me.\n\nSometimes in the classroom, if the fluorescent lights are buzzing and the kids are all talking at once, I can feel sort of… worn thin, I guess. Like I’m focusing on one thing and missing three others, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I forgot where I put the markers—actually, last week I put them in the art cabinet and then spent ten minutes looking in the supply bin, which is ridiculous.\n\nBut that’s not the world seeming not real. It’s just me being scattered or overstimulated. So, no, not distant or unreal like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other physical symptoms that persist, even after medical evaluations or tests have shown no clear medical cause for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah—well, I don’t know if this counts exactly, but the big thing has been my shoulders and neck. They just stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends.\n\nAnd it’s funny, because actual observations are usually fine—I mean, stressful, but fine—and I’ve been teaching long enough that you’d think I wouldn’t react like that. But even on ordinary days, I catch myself with my shoulders up around my ears. By the time I get home, my jaw aches too, and sometimes it’s bad enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar, which is one of the few things that usually helps me settle down.\n\nSo yes, that tension is honestly part of what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other physical symptoms, besides the tightness in your shoulders and neck, that have persisted even after seeing a doctor and having tests done that didn’t find a clear medical explanation?\n\nPatient: Um… besides the shoulders and neck, I don’t think there’s anything else like that that’s really persisted.\n\nI mean, I’ve had the usual things people get, I guess, where you wonder if something is wrong and then it turns out to be nothing, but nothing where I’ve gone through a bunch of tests and they still couldn’t explain it. The tension is the thing that sticks around.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I just know once you start paying attention to your body, everything can feel like it might mean something—especially when you’re around little kids all day and someone is always announcing they have a stomachache five minutes after snack. But for me, no, not really anything else that’s ongoing besides that tightness.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a lot of anxiety or concern about your physical symptoms, like worrying they might mean something more serious even when doctors haven’t found a clear explanation for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in that way.\n\nI mean, I can get fixated on it for a minute, like, “Why am I still so tense?” and then I’ll start thinking about whether I’m doing something wrong—sitting wrong, sleeping wrong, carrying too many bins at school, that kind of thing. I actually bought one of those heat wraps after seeing another teacher use one during nap time, which is probably not the point of your question.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been scared it’s something serious after a doctor said they weren’t finding anything. It’s more frustrating than frightening, if that makes sense. Like my body won’t stop bracing, but I’m not usually thinking, “Oh no, this must be some terrible illness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling especially anxious or worried specifically about your physical symptoms, like your tightness, even when doctors haven’t found a serious cause for them?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least I don’t think that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI get annoyed by it, and sometimes I sort of chase it around in my head—like, “Am I sitting funny? Did I sleep wrong? Is this from bending over tiny tables all day?” You know, those preschool chairs are basically designed to ruin an adult spine, not that that’s a medical theory or anything.\n\nBut I’m not usually worrying that the tightness means something serious. It’s more like… why can’t I just relax my body for five minutes? That’s the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that your physical symptoms, like the tightness in your shoulders and neck, might mean you have a serious medical condition that hasn’t been diagnosed yet, even after doctors have told you they can’t find anything serious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that.\n\nI mean, I can spiral a little in a general way, because once you notice your shoulders are up around your ears all day, you start thinking, “Okay, what am I doing to myself?” But I’m not usually sitting there thinking there’s some hidden serious diagnosis that everyone missed.\n\nIt’s more… I wish I could unclench. Like even when I’m trying to relax, my body doesn’t seem to get the message. And then of course I’ll get distracted wondering if I need a better pillow or if those tiny classroom chairs should come with a warning label for adults. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m worried it’s an undiagnosed serious condition.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about your physical symptoms, like how serious they might be or what they could mean, even when doctors have said there’s no major issue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I spend a lot of time on what it “means,” exactly. I notice it, sure, and then sometimes I’ll get sidetracked trying to figure out if it’s from how I’m sitting or how I’m moving around at work.\n\nLike, I’ll be on the floor with the kids doing blocks, and then later I’m thinking, “Well, no wonder my body’s mad at me,” because those little chairs—honestly, they should issue adults knee pads or something. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I’m not sitting there for hours thinking it might be serious or anything like that. It’s more like it pops into my head, I get irritated by it, and then I move on to whatever else is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of anxiety or fear that your physical symptoms, like your tightness, might mean something very serious—even if doctors haven’t found anything wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear that it’s something very serious, no.\n\nI mean, I notice the tightness and then my brain starts wandering around it—like whether I’ve been hunched over at the art table too long, or carrying supplies in some ridiculous way, or sitting in those tiny chairs again. Those chairs are… honestly, I don’t know how anyone over the age of five is supposed to survive them.\n\nBut the “what if it’s something terrible” thing? That’s not really where I go with it. It’s more frustration, I guess. Like, why is my body stuck like this, why can’t I just loosen up and get on with the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of anxiety or fear that your physical symptoms, like your tightness, might mean something very serious—even if doctors haven’t found anything wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it fear, exactly. I know you asked that already, and I’m trying to answer it the right way.\n\nIt’s more that I notice the tightness and then I get annoyed by it, and my mind starts making a little list—was it the chairs, was I leaning over the tables, did I carry too much stuff from the supply closet. The supply closet is its own whole situation, by the way, because every time I go in there looking for one thing, I come out holding twelve things and somehow no tape.\n\nBut no, I don’t really have a clear “this must be something very serious” thought. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more frustration, I guess. Like, why is my body stuck like this, why can’t I just loosen up and get on with the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a lot of anxiety or fear that your physical symptoms, like your tightness, might mean something very serious—even if doctors haven’t found anything wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it fear, exactly. I know you asked that already, and I’m trying to answer it the right way.\n\nIt’s more that I notice the tightness and then I get annoyed by it, and my mind starts making a little list—was it the chairs, was I leaning over the tables, did I carry too much stuff from the supply closet. The supply closet is its own whole situation, by the way, because every time I go in there looking for one thing, I come out holding twelve things and somehow no tape.\n\nBut no, I don’t really have a clear “this must be something very serious” thought. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that."
